**Objective**: Generate a semantically equivalent vectorize-friendly version of `*_opt` using:  
**Preservation Requirements**:  
1. Function signature

If the original value is used, e.g. a[i+1] is used for iteration i, a[i+1] should be stored in a temporary variable(use memcpy to store orign value) or used before updating a[i+1]
If the updated value is used, e.g. a[i-1] is used for iteration i, a[i-1] should update before using it.

**Possible Transformations**  
Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body  
   - Example:  
     ```c
     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed
     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  
     for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  
     ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops  

Use temporary variable:
Use temp variable to store result to avoid complex memory access

Remove branching logic (if, else if, switch) from loops to enable automatic vectorization:
Replace multi-way conditionals with arithmetic masking, ensuring uniform execution paths across iterations.
**When to Apply**:  
    - Example:  
     ```c
    // Original
    if (cond1)  
        x = value_A;  
    else if (cond2)  
        x = value_B;  
    else  
        x = value_C;
    // Optimized  
    x = cond1 * value_A + (cond2 && !cond1) * value_B + (!cond1 && !cond2) * value_C;  
     ```

If the access pattern involves using a[i-1] and updating a[i] within the same iteration, a dependency exists. Attempt to reorder the instructions to resolve this dependency.

## FEEDBACK OUTPUT
## Analysis  

### 1. **Compilation Errors in Optimized Code**  
The optimized code compiles without syntax errors.  
The compiler feedback is a remark about vectorization, not a compilation error, so we proceed.

---

### 2. **Semantic Comparison**  

**Source code behavior:**  
- Outer loop runs `nl` times (depends on `iterations`).  
- For each `nl`:  
  - `k = -1`  
  - Loops over `i` (columns of `bb`), then `j` (rows of `bb`).  
  - If `bb[j][i] > 0`, increment `k` and store `aa[j][i]` into `flat_2d_array[k]`.  
- This means `flat_2d_array` is filled **row-major with respect to `bb`** (since `j` changes faster in inner loop), but only for positive `bb` entries.  
- `k` increments globally across all `i` for each `nl`.

**Optimized code behavior:**  
- For each `i`, it collects positive `bb` entries into `temp_store[temp_count]` from `j = 0..255`.  
- Then copies `temp_store` into `flat_2d_array` starting from `local_k = k` (value at start of this `i`).  
- After processing all `j` for this `i`, `k` is updated to `local_k`.  

**Semantic equivalence check:**  
Let’s test with a small example (2×2 instead of 256×256 for brevity).  

Suppose:  
```
bb = [ [1, -1],
       [2,  0] ]
aa = [ [10, 20],
       [30, 40] ]
```
Source code (i=0, j=0..1):  
- `bb[0][0]=1>0` → k=0, flat[0]=aa[0][0]=10  
- `bb[1][0]=2>0` → k=1, flat[1]=aa[1][0]=30  
(i=1, j=0..1):  
- `bb[0][1]=-1>0?` no  
- `bb[1][1]=0>0?` no  
Result: flat = [10, 30]  

Optimized code:  
i=0: temp_store collects aa[0][0]=10, aa[1][0]=30 → temp_count=2, local_k starts -1, after copy local_k=1, k=1.  
i=1: temp_store collects nothing (temp_count=0), local_k stays 1, k=1.  
flat[0..1] = [10, 30] ✅  

The order of writes to `flat_2d_array` is the same as source because within each `i`, `j` runs sequentially, and `temp_store` preserves that order.  
Thus semantics match.

---

### 3. **Vectorization Analysis**  

Compiler remark:  
> loop not vectorized: value that could not be identified as reduction is used outside the loop  

This refers to the inner `j` loop in optimized code:  
```c
for (int j = 0; j < 256; j++) {
    if (bb[j][i] > (float)0.) {
        temp_store[temp_count++] = aa[j][i];
    }
}
```
`temp_count` is a scalar that changes conditionally inside the loop — it’s a form of **compression** (gather-like), not a simple reduction.  
The compiler cannot vectorize this easily because:  
- The store to `temp_store` is indexed by `temp_count`, which depends on runtime condition.  
- Vectorizing would require masked stores and compacting, which the compiler may not deduce automatically.  

**Why vectorization fails:**  
The loop has a **loop-carried dependency** on `temp_count` (each iteration increments it conditionally, and the increment is data-dependent on `bb`).  
This is not a recognized reduction pattern for the compiler in this context.

**Potential improvement to enable vectorization:**  
We could try to compute a mask array first (vectorizable comparison), then use a prefix sum on the mask to compute indices for scattering `aa` values into `temp_store`.  
But here, `temp_store` is a small temporary, so maybe we can let compiler vectorize the comparison and use SIMD to populate `temp_store` via scalar code? Actually, compilers like ICC or Clang with `-O3` can sometimes vectorize such compress loops using AVX2/AVX-512 masked gathers/scatters, but the remark suggests it failed here.

Given the remark, the current optimized code **is not auto-vectorized** for the inner `j` loop, so there is **optimization opportunity**.

---

### 4. **Conclusion**  

- No compilation error.  
- Semantics match.  
- Vectorization failed due to conditional index increment.  
- Opportunity exists to improve vectorization (e.g., using masked stores in AVX-512, or loop transformation to separate mask generation and compaction).  

Since vectorization is not achieved and there’s an opportunity for improvement, the result is **FAIL**.

---

**Final:** FAIL

If the original value is used, e.g. a[i+1] is used for iteration i, a[i+1] should be stored in a temporary variable(use memcpy to store orign value) or used before updating a[i+1]
If the updated value is used, e.g. a[i-1] is used for iteration i, a[i-1] should update before using it.

**Possible Transformations**  
Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body  
   - Example:  
     ```c
     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed
     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  
     for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  
     ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops  

Use temporary variable:
Use temp variable to store result to avoid complex memory access

Remove branching logic (if, else if, switch) from loops to enable automatic vectorization:
Replace multi-way conditionals with arithmetic masking, ensuring uniform execution paths across iterations.
**When to Apply**:  
    - Example:  
     ```c
    // Original
    if (cond1)  
        x = value_A;  
    else if (cond2)  
        x = value_B;  
    else  
        x = value_C;
    // Optimized  
    x = cond1 * value_A + (cond2 && !cond1) * value_B + (!cond1 && !cond2) * value_C;  
     ```

If the access pattern involves using a[i-1] and updating a[i] within the same iteration, a dependency exists. Attempt to reorder the instructions to resolve this dependency.

void s343_opt(int iterations, float *flat_2d_array, float aa[256][256], float bb[256][256]) {
    int k;
    for (int nl = 0; nl < 10 * (iterations / 256); nl++) {
        k = -1;
        for (int i = 0; i < 256; i++) {
            int local_k = k;
            float temp_store[256];
            int temp_count = 0;
            
            // Generate mask and collect values in separate steps
            int mask[256];
            float values[256];
            
            // Vector-friendly mask generation
            for (int j = 0; j < 256; j++) {
                mask[j] = bb[j][i] > (float)0.;
            }
            
            // Collect values based on mask
            for (int j = 0; j < 256; j++) {
                if (mask[j]) {
                    values[temp_count] = aa[j][i];
                    temp_count++;
                }
            }
            
            // Copy to final array
            for (int t = 0; t < temp_count; t++) {
                flat_2d_array[++local_k] = values[t];
            }
            k = local_k;
        }
    }
}

**Objective**: Generate a semantically equivalent vectorize-friendly version of `*_opt` using:  
**Preservation Requirements**:  
1. Function signature

If the original value is used, e.g. a[i+1] is used for iteration i, a[i+1] should be stored in a temporary variable(use memcpy to store orign value) or used before updating a[i+1]
If the updated value is used, e.g. a[i-1] is used for iteration i, a[i-1] should update before using it.

**Possible Transformations**  
Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body  
   - Example:  
     ```c
     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed
     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  
     for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  
     ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops  

Use temporary variable:
Use temp variable to store result to avoid complex memory access

Remove branching logic (if, else if, switch) from loops to enable automatic vectorization:
Replace multi-way conditionals with arithmetic masking, ensuring uniform execution paths across iterations.
**When to Apply**:  
    - Example:  
     ```c
    // Original
    if (cond1)  
        x = value_A;  
    else if (cond2)  
        x = value_B;  
    else  
        x = value_C;
    // Optimized  
    x = cond1 * value_A + (cond2 && !cond1) * value_B + (!cond1 && !cond2) * value_C;  
     ```

Let’s go through the stages step by step.  

---

### **1. Compilation Errors in Optimized Code**  
The optimized code compiles without errors (the compiler feedback shows remarks, not errors).  
We can proceed to semantic comparison.

---

### **2. Semantic Comparison with Simple Test Cases**  

The original code:  
- For each `(i, j)` in column-major order (`i` outer, `j` inner), if `bb[j][i] > 0`, then `flat_2d_array[++k] = aa[j][i]`.  
- This writes elements in **column-major order** of `aa` and `bb`, but only when the condition is met, and packs them into `flat_2d_array` sequentially.

The optimized code:  
- For each `i` (column), it first computes a mask for all `j` (0..255) and stores `aa[j][i]` into `temp_values[j]`.  
- Then it computes `write_indices[j]` based on prefix sum of the mask, but **only within that column**.  
- Then it scatters `temp_values[j]` to `flat_2d_array[write_indices[j]]`.  

Key difference:  
In the original, `k` is global across all columns — after processing column `i=0`, `k` continues for column `i=1`, so `flat_2d_array` is filled with all qualifying elements from all columns in one contiguous sequence.  

In the optimized version, for each column it resets `local_k = k` (starting point for that column), computes `count` for that column, then `k = local_k + count`. This is **correct** — the `k` update is the same as original, because in original `k` increments across columns too.  

But let’s test with a tiny example:  
Suppose `bb` has `>0` at `(j=0,i=0)` and `(j=0,i=1)`.  

Original:  
`i=0, j=0`: `k=0`, `flat[0]=aa[0][0]`  
`i=0, j=1..255`: no more positives in column 0.  
`i=1, j=0`: `k=1`, `flat[1]=aa[0][1]`.  

Optimized:  
`i=0`: `local_k=-1`, mask[0]=1, others 0, count=1, write_indices[0]=0, scatter: `flat[0]=aa[0][0]`, `k=-1+1=0`.  
`i=1`: `local_k=0`, mask[0]=1, count=1, write_indices[0]=1, scatter: `flat[1]=aa[0][1]`, `k=0+1=1`.  

Same result.  

So **semantics are preserved**.  

---

### **3. Vectorization Analysis**  

Compiler feedback:  
- First loop (`for (int j=0; j<256; j++)` computing mask and temp_values):  
  Remark: "the cost-model indicates that vectorization is not beneficial" but also "interleaved loop (interleaved count: 2)".  
  This means it was interleaved but not SIMD vectorized (maybe due to small trip count or overhead).  

- Second loop (prefix sum on mask):  
  "loop not vectorized: value that could not be identified as reduction is used outside the loop" — this is because `count` is used after the loop (to update `k`), but `count` is a reduction (sum of mask). Actually, `count` is a reduction, but `write_indices[j]` depends on `count` incrementally — that’s a **prefix sum**, which is harder to vectorize automatically.  

- Third loop (scatter):  
  "vectorized loop (vectorization width: 4, interleaved count: 1)" — scatter is vectorized.  

The main blocker for full vectorization is the **prefix sum loop** (second loop), which has a loop-carried dependency (`count` updates sequentially).  

---

### **4. Optimization Opportunities**  

The optimized code introduced extra passes (3 passes per column) and extra arrays (`mask`, `temp_values`, `write_indices`), which increases memory operations.  
The original loop could be vectorized with a **masked gather/scatter** if targeting AVX-512, but here the compiler didn’t vectorize the original due to the unpredictable scatter.  

However, the optimized version’s prefix sum is the bottleneck and not auto-vectorized.  

Better approach:  
- Use SIMD to compute mask, compute prefix sum within SIMD registers (vplzcntd + vpermd for prefix sum in AVX-512), then vectorized scatter.  
But since the compiler can’t auto-vectorize the prefix sum, **the optimized code is not fully optimized** — there’s an opportunity to improve.  

Given the feedback, the optimized code still has a non-vectorized loop (prefix sum), so it’s **not fully optimized**.  

---

**Final decision:**  
FAIL — because there is an optimization opportunity (prefix sum loop not vectorized).

        k = -1;
        for (int i = 0; i < 256; i++) {
            int local_k = k;
            int mask[256];
            float temp_values[256];
            
            // First pass: compute mask and store values
            for (int j = 0; j < 256; j++) {
                mask[j] = bb[j][i] > (float)0.;
                temp_values[j] = aa[j][i];
            }
            
            // Second pass: compute write indices using prefix sum
            int write_indices[256];
            int count = 0;
            for (int j = 0; j < 256; j++) {
                if (mask[j]) {
                    write_indices[j] = local_k + 1 + count;
                    count++;
                } else {
                    write_indices[j] = -1;
                }
            }
            
            // Third pass: scatter values using computed indices
            for (int j = 0; j < 256; j++) {
                int idx = write_indices[j];
                if (idx != -1) {
                    flat_2d_array[idx] = temp_values[j];
                }
            }
            
            k = local_k + count;
        }
    }
}

```

### **2. Semantic Comparison with Simple Test Cases**  

The original code:  
- For each `(i, j)` in column-major order (`i` outer, `j` inner), if `bb[j][i] > 0`, then `flat_2d_array[++k] = aa[j][i]`.  
- This writes elements in **column-major order** of `aa` and `bb`, but only when the condition is met, and packs them into `flat_2d_array` sequentially.

The optimized code:  
- For each `i` (column), it first computes a mask for all `j` (0..255) and stores `aa[j][i]` into `temp_values[j]`.  
- Then it computes `write_indices[j]` based on prefix sum of the mask, but **only within that column**.  
- Then it scatters `temp_values[j]` to `flat_2d_array[write_indices[j]]`.  

Key difference:  
In the original, `k` is global across all columns — after processing column `i=0`, `k` continues for column `i=1`, so `flat_2d_array` is filled with all qualifying elements from all columns in one contiguous sequence.  

In the optimized version, for each column it resets `local_k = k` (starting point for that column), computes `count` for that column, then `k = local_k + count`. This is **correct** — the `k` update is the same as original, because in original `k` increments across columns too.  

But let’s test with a tiny example:  
Suppose `bb` has `>0` at `(j=0,i=0)` and `(j=0,i=1)`.  

Original:  
`i=0, j=0`: `k=0`, `flat[0]=aa[0][0]`  
`i=0, j=1..255`: no more positives in column 0.  
`i=1, j=0`: `k=1`, `flat[1]=aa[0][1]`.  

Optimized:  
`i=0`: `local_k=-1`, mask[0]=1, others 0, count=1, write_indices[0]=0, scatter: `flat[0]=aa[0][0]`, `k=-1+1=0`.  
`i=1`: `local_k=0`, mask[0]=1, count=1, write_indices[0]=1, scatter: `flat[1]=aa[0][1]`, `k=0+1=1`.  

Same result.  

So **semantics are preserved**.

Compiler feedback:  
- First loop (`for (int j=0; j<256; j++)` computing mask and temp_values):  
  Remark: "the cost-model indicates that vectorization is not beneficial" but also "interleaved loop (interleaved count: 2)".  
  This means it was interleaved but not SIMD vectorized (maybe due to small trip count or overhead).  

- Second loop (prefix sum on mask):  
  "loop not vectorized: value that could not be identified as reduction is used outside the loop" — this is because `count` is used after the loop (to update `k`), but `count` is a reduction (sum of mask). Actually, `count` is a reduction, but `write_indices[j]` depends on `count` incrementally — that’s a **prefix sum**, which is harder to vectorize automatically.  

- Third loop (scatter):  
  "vectorized loop (vectorization width: 4, interleaved count: 1)" — scatter is vectorized.  

The main blocker for full vectorization is the **prefix sum loop** (second loop), which has a loop-carried dependency (`count` updates sequentially).  

---

### **4. Optimization Opportunities**  

The optimized code introduced extra passes (3 passes per column) and extra arrays (`mask`, `temp_values`, `write_indices`), which increases memory operations.  
The original loop could be vectorized with a **masked gather/scatter** if targeting AVX-512, but here the compiler didn’t vectorize the original due to the unpredictable scatter.  

However, the optimized version’s prefix sum is the bottleneck and not auto-vectorized.  

Better approach:  
- Use SIMD to compute mask, compute prefix sum within SIMD registers (vplzcntd + vpermd for prefix sum in AVX-512), then vectorized scatter.  
But since the compiler can’t auto-vectorize the prefix sum, **the optimized code is not fully optimized** — there’s an opportunity to improve.  

Given the feedback, the optimized code still has a non-vectorized loop (prefix sum), so it’s **not fully optimized**.

**Possible Transformations**  
Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body  
   - Example:  
     ```c
     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed
     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  
     for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  
     ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops  

Use temporary variable:
Use temp variable to store result to avoid complex memory access

Replace multi-way conditionals with arithmetic masking, ensuring uniform execution paths across iterations.

     ```c
    // Original
    if (cond1)  
        x = value_A;  
    else if (cond2)  
        x = value_B;  
    else  
        x = value_C;
    // Optimized  
    x = cond1 * value_A + (cond2 && !cond1) * value_B + (!cond1 && !cond2) * value_C;

- Then it computes `write_indices[j]` as `local_k + 1 + count` where `count` is the number of `mask=1` so far in this column.
- Then it scatters using `write_indices[j]`.

3. `write_indices[j]` for positives = `local_k + 1 + prefix_sum_of_mask_up_to_j`, which is exactly the `k` value at that point in original loop.

1. Function signature

If the original value is used, e.g. a[i+1] is used for iteration i, a[i+1] should be stored in a temporary variable(use memcpy to store orign value) or used before updating a[i+1]
If the updated value is used, e.g. a[i-1] is used for iteration i, a[i-1] should update before using it.

**Possible Transformations**  
Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body  
   - Example:  
     ```c
     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed
     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  
     for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  
     ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops  

Use temporary variable:
Use temp variable to store result to avoid complex memory access

Remove branching logic (if, else if, switch) from loops to enable automatic vectorization:
Replace multi-way conditionals with arithmetic masking, ensuring uniform execution paths across iterations.
**When to Apply**:  
    - Example:  
     ```c
    // Original
    if (cond1)  
        x = value_A;  
    else if (cond2)  
        x = value_B;  
    else  
        x = value_C;
    // Optimized  
    x = cond1 * value_A + (cond2 && !cond1) * value_B + (!cond1 && !cond2) * value_C;  
     ```

void s343_opt(int iterations, float *flat_2d_array, float aa[256][256], float bb[256][256]) {
    for (int nl = 0; nl < 10 * (iterations / 256); nl++) {
        int k = -1;
        for (int i = 0; i < 256; i++) {
            int local_k = k;
            int mask[256];
            float temp_values[256];
            
            // First pass: compute mask and store values (no dependencies)
            for (int j = 0; j < 256; j++) {
                mask[j] = bb[j][i] > (float)0.;
                temp_values[j] = aa[j][i];
            }
            
            // Second pass: compute prefix sum (sequential but short)
            int prefix_sum[256];
            int count = 0;
            for (int j = 0; j < 256; j++) {
                if (mask[j]) {
                    prefix_sum[j] = count;
                    count++;
                } else {
                    prefix_sum[j] = -1;
                }
            }
            
            // Third pass: write results using computed indices (vectorizable)
            for (int j = 0; j < 256; j++) {
                int idx = prefix_sum[j];
                if (idx != -1) {
                    flat_2d_array[local_k + 1 + idx] = temp_values[j];
                }
            }
            
            k = local_k + count;
        }
    }
}

The optimized code attempts to:

   - Third pass: Write results using computed indices

**Potential Issue:** The optimized code processes each column independently with `local_k = k`, but the source code's `k` accumulates across ALL columns, not resetting per column. The optimized code correctly handles this by:

**Step-by-step verification** confirms the semantics match:

```c
void s343_opt(int iterations, float *flat_2d_array, float aa[256][256], float bb[256][256]) {
    for (int nl = 0; nl < 10 * (iterations / 256); nl++) {
        int k = -1;
        
        // Process all columns
        for (int i = 0; i < 256; i++) {
            int local_k = k;
            int write_positions[256];
            float temp_values[256];
            
            // First pass: compute mask and store values (vectorizable)
            // This can be vectorized with masked operations
            for (int j = 0; j < 256; j++) {
                int mask = bb[j][i] > (float)0.;
                temp_values[j] = aa[j][i];
                write_positions[j] = mask;
            }
            
            // Second pass: convert mask to write indices using parallel prefix sum
            // We'll use a two-phase approach to avoid sequential dependency
            int block_size = 8; // SIMD-friendly size
            int block_counts[256/8 + 1] = {0};
            
            // Phase 1: Count positives in each block (vectorizable)
            for (int block = 0; block < 256; block += block_size) {
                int block_count = 0;
                for (int j = block; j < block + block_size && j < 256; j++) {
                    block_count += write_positions[j];
                }
                block_counts[block/block_size] = block_count;
            }
            
            // Phase 2: Compute prefix sum of block counts (short sequential)
            int total_count = 0;
            for (int b = 0; b < 256/block_size; b++) {

                        block_offset++;
                    }
                }
            }
            
            k = local_k + total_count;
        }

void s343_opt(int iterations, float *flat_2d_array, float aa[256][256], float bb[256][256]) {
    for (int nl = 0; nl < 10 * (iterations / 256); nl++) {

                temp_values[j] = aa[j][i];
                write_positions[j] = mask;

            // We'll use a two-phase approach to avoid sequential dependency
            int block_size = 8; // SIMD-friendly size

            // Phase 1: Count positives in each block (vectorizable)

                total_count += block_counts[b];
                block_counts[b] = old_total; // Store prefix sum for this block

            // Phase 3: Compute final positions within each block (vectorizable)
            for (int block = 0; block < 256; block += block_size) {

                    if (write_positions[j]) {
                        flat_2d_array[local_k + 1 + block_offset] = temp_values[j];
                        block_offset++;
                    }

            }
            
            k = local_k + total_count;

    }

### 2. **Semantic Comparison with Simple Test Cases**
**FAIL - Semantic differences detected**

The optimized code has significant semantic differences from the source code:

- Source code: Processes `bb[j][i]` and `aa[j][i]` with `j` as the inner loop (column-major access for `bb`)
- Optimized code: Processes `bb[j][i]` with `j` as inner loop (correct), but uses `temp_values[j] = aa[j][i]` which loads all values regardless of condition

**Key Issue 2: Write position calculation is flawed**
The optimized code attempts to use parallel prefix sum but has several problems:
1. It stores all `aa[j][i]` values in `temp_values[j]` unconditionally
2. The prefix sum calculation doesn't properly compute the final write positions
3. The final write uses `local_k + 1 + block_offset` which doesn't match the source code's sequential `k++` behavior

**Example Test Case Analysis:**
Let's trace with a simple 2x2 example:
```
aa = [[1, 2], [3, 4]]

- i=0, j=1: bb[1][0]=1 → k=0, flat_2d_array[0]=aa[1][0]=3
- i=1, j=0: bb[0][1]=1 → k=1, flat_2d_array[1]=aa[0][1]=2
- i=1, j=1: bb[1][1]=0 → skip

The complex prefix sum logic doesn't produce the same sequential write pattern. The algorithm fundamentally changes how elements are selected and written.

The source code has a **true dependency** on `k` (read-after-write in `k++`), which creates a sequential bottleneck. The optimized code attempts to parallelize this with prefix sums but:
1. Fails to maintain the exact write order (though order may not matter if only values matter)
2. Has implementation errors in the prefix sum calculation
3. Unnecessarily loads all `aa` values instead of conditionally loading

### **Recommendations for Fixing the Optimized Code**
1. **Simplify the approach**: Instead of complex prefix sums, consider:
   - Using compiler pragmas for vectorization with reduction
   - Processing in blocks where `k` is updated per block
   - Using scatter operations if available in the target architecture

2. **Maintain conditional loading**: Only load `aa[j][i]` when `bb[j][i] > 0`

3. **Fix the prefix sum implementation**: If keeping the prefix sum approach, ensure it correctly computes write positions.

**The optimized code needs to be rewritten to match the source code's semantics before any vectorization optimization can be considered.**

**Objective**: Generate a semantically equivalent vectorize-friendly version of `*_opt` using:  
**Preservation Requirements**:  
1. Function signature

If the original value is used, e.g. a[i+1] is used for iteration i, a[i+1] should be stored in a temporary variable(use memcpy to store orign value) or used before updating a[i+1]
If the updated value is used, e.g. a[i-1] is used for iteration i, a[i-1] should update before using it.

**Possible Transformations**  
Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body  
   - Example:  
     ```c
     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed
     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  
     for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  
     ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops  

Use temporary variable:
Use temp variable to store result to avoid complex memory access

Remove branching logic (if, else if, switch) from loops to enable automatic vectorization:
Replace multi-way conditionals with arithmetic masking, ensuring uniform execution paths across iterations.
**When to Apply**:  
    - Example:  
     ```c
    // Original
    if (cond1)  
        x = value_A;  
    else if (cond2)  
        x = value_B;  
    else  
        x = value_C;
    // Optimized  
    x = cond1 * value_A + (cond2 && !cond1) * value_B + (!cond1 && !cond2) * value_C;  
     ```

If the access pattern involves using a[i-1] and updating a[i] within the same iteration, a dependency exists. Attempt to reorder the instructions to resolve this dependency.

## FEEDBACK OUTPUT
## Analysis Report  

### 1. Compilation Errors in Optimized Code  
The optimized code compiles without errors.  

### 2. Semantic Comparison with Simple Test Cases  
The optimized code **does not** preserve the original semantics.  

**Reason:**  
- In the original code, `k` is a single global index across all columns.  
- In the optimized code, `k` is updated per column (`local_k` starts from `k` for each column), but the writes to `flat_2d_array` are still sequential across columns because `local_k` is reset to `k` at the start of each column loop.  
- However, there is a **critical bug**: The `mask` array is computed for each column, but the `local_k` loop writes to `flat_2d_array` using `local_k + 1` as the index. This means that within a column, the indices are sequential, but **between columns**, the starting index for the second column should be `k + 1` from the previous column, not resetting to `k` and then incrementing.  

Let’s test with a small example:

- Column 1: row 1 → `aa[1][1] = 2.0`

- `k` starts at -1.  
- Column 0: `k` becomes 0, `flat_2d_array[0] = 1.0`.  
- Column 1: `k` is now 0, then increments to 1, `flat_2d_array[1] = 2.0`.  
Result: `[1.0, 2.0]`.  

**Optimized code:**  
- `k = -1`.  
- Column 0: `local_k = -1`, loop writes `flat_2d_array[0] = 1.0`, `local_k = 0`, `k = 0`.  
- Column 1: `local_k = k = 0`, loop writes `flat_2d_array[1] = 2.0`, `local_k = 1`, `k = 1`.  
Result: `[1.0, 2.0]` → **same in this case**.  

But consider if `bb` has two positives in column 0 and one in column 1:  
- Column 0: rows 0 and 1 positive → `aa[0][0]=1.0`, `aa[1][0]=3.0`.  
- Column 1: row 0 positive → `aa[0][1]=2.0`.

- Column 1: `k=1` → `k=2`, `flat[2]=2.0`.  
Result: `[1.0, 3.0, 2.0]`.

- Column 1: `local_k = 1`, loop writes `flat[2]=2.0`, `local_k=2`, `k=2`.  
Result: `[1.0, 3.0, 2.0]` → **still same**.  

Wait — the bug is not in the indexing between columns, because `k` is updated after each column. The real issue is **dependence on `k`** inside the inner loop. In the original, `k++` is done **inside** the `j` loop, but in the optimized version, `local_k` is updated inside the second `j` loop. This is fine because `local_k` is private to the column.  

But there is a **subtle bug**: In the optimized version, the `mask` array is computed in the first inner loop, but if `bb[j][i]` changes between the first and second inner loops (not possible here since `bb` is read-only in this scope), there could be inconsistency.     for (int nl = 0; nl < 10 * (iterations / 256); nl++) {
        int k = -1;
        
        // Process each column independently
        for (int i = 0; i < 256; i++) {
            // Temporary arrays for this column
            float temp_vals[256];
            int mask[256];

                    flat_2d_array[local_k + 1] = temp_vals[j];

**Preservation Requirements**:  
1. Function signature

**Possible Transformations**  
Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body  
   - Example:  
     ```c
     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed
     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  
     for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  
     ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops  

Use temporary variable:
Use temp variable to store result to avoid complex memory access

Remove branching logic (if, else if, switch) from loops to enable automatic vectorization:
Replace multi-way conditionals with arithmetic masking, ensuring uniform execution paths across iterations.
**When to Apply**:  
    - Example:  
     ```c
    // Original
    if (cond1)  
        x = value_A;  
    else if (cond2)  
        x = value_B;  
    else  
        x = value_C;
    // Optimized  
    x = cond1 * value_A + (cond2 && !cond1) * value_B + (!cond1 && !cond2) * value_C;  
     ```

```c
void s343_opt(int iterations, float *flat_2d_array, float aa[256][256], float bb[256][256]) {
    for (int nl = 0; nl < 10 * (iterations / 256); nl++) {
        int k = -1;

            float temp_vals[256];
            int mask[256];
            int indices[256];

            for (int j = 0; j < 256; j++) {
                mask[j] = (bb[j][i] > (float)0.);
                temp_vals[j] = aa[j][i];
            }
            
            // Second loop: compute prefix sum of mask to get indices (vectorizable)

            for (int j = 0; j < 256; j++) {

                    prefix_sum++;
                }

            // Third loop: write selected values sequentially (vectorizable load)
            for (int idx = 0; idx < prefix_sum; idx++) {
                flat_2d_array[k + 1 + idx] = temp_vals[indices[idx]];
            }
            
            // Update global k
            k += prefix_sum;
        }
    }
}

Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body  
   - Example:  
     ```c
     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed
     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  
     for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  
     ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops

}

- Then it computes a **prefix sum** of the mask for that column only, but incorrectly:

            for (int j = 0; j < 256; j += 8) {
                // Process 8 elements at a time
                int local_sum = 0;
                for (int jj = 0; jj < 8 && j + jj < 256; jj++) {
                    local_sum += mask[j + jj];
                }
                // Update global counter
                prefix_sum += local_sum;
            }
            
            // Third loop: write selected values using gather pattern
            int write_idx = k + 1;
            for (int j = 0; j < 256; j++) {
                if (mask[j]) {
                    flat_2d_array[write_idx] = temp_vals[j];
                    write_idx++;
                }
            }
            
            // Update global k
            k += prefix_sum;
        }
    }
}

## **2. Semantic Comparison with Simple Test Cases**

The original code:

- Loops over `i` (outer), then `j` (inner), but accesses `bb[j][i]` and `aa[j][i]` — meaning `i` is column index, `j` is row index.
- `k` increments globally across all `i` and `j` as positive elements are found in `bb`.
- `flat_2d_array` is filled in row-major order of `j` within each `i`.

The optimized code:

- For each column `i`, it computes a mask `mask[j] = (bb[j][i] > 0)` and stores `temp_vals[j] = aa[j][i]`.
- Then it computes a **prefix sum** of the mask for that column only, but incorrectly:  
  It sums `mask[j]` for the column, but doesn’t compute per-element prefix sum for writing within that column.  
  Instead, it uses `write_idx = k + 1` and increments `write_idx` for each mask true, but `k` is not updated until after the column is processed.  
  This means `write_idx` starts at `k+1` for each column, but `k` is only updated after the column is done — this is **wrong** because `k` should be updated **as we write** across columns, not after.

Let’s test with a tiny example:

Suppose `bb` is 2×2:  
`bb[0][0] = 1`, `bb[1][0] = 0`, `bb[0][1] = 0`, `bb[1][1] = 1`.  
`aa` same as `bb` for simplicity.

**Original code** (i=0, j=0 → k=0, write aa[0][0]; i=0, j=1 skip; i=1, j=0 skip; i=1, j=1 → k=1, write aa[1][1])  
→ `flat_2d_array[0] = aa[0][0]`, `flat_2d_array[1] = aa[1][1]`.

**Optimized code**:

- Column i=0: mask[0]=1, mask[1]=0, temp_vals[0]=aa[0][0], temp_vals[1]=aa[1][0].  
  prefix_sum = 1.  
  write_idx = k+1 = -1+1 = 0.  
  j=0: mask[0]=1 → write flat[0]=aa[0][0], write_idx=1.  
  j=1: mask[1]=0 → skip.  
  k += prefix_sum → k = -1+1 = 0.

- Column i=1: mask[0]=0, mask[1]=1, temp_vals[0]=aa[0][1], temp_vals[1]=aa[1][1].  
  prefix_sum = 1.  
  write_idx = k+1 = 0+1 = 1.  
  j=0: mask[0]=0 → skip.  
  j=1: mask[1]=1 → write flat[1]=aa[1][1], write_idx=2.  
  k += prefix_sum → k=1.

Result matches original in this case. Let’s check another case where order matters within a column:

Actually, within each column, the original writes in increasing `j` order, and optimized does the same (loop j=0..255 writing if mask[j]). So order is preserved.

But `k` update: original increments `k` immediately when condition true, so `k` is current write index. Optimized uses `write_idx = k+1` at start of column, writes, then `k += prefix_sum`. Since `write_idx` starts at `k+1` and increments for each true mask, final `write_idx` after column = `k+1 + prefix_sum`. Then `k` becomes `k + prefix_sum`, which equals `write_idx - 1`. This matches original’s `k` after column.

So semantics seem preserved.  

But there’s a **bug**: `write_idx` is reset for each `j` loop, but `write_idx` is local to the column loop, so it’s fine. Wait, `write_idx` is declared inside column loop, reinitialized each column. Yes.

Thus semantic equivalence holds.

## **3. Vectorization Analysis**

Compiler feedback says:

1. First inner loop (`for (int j=0; j<256; j++)` computing mask and temp_vals) is not vectorized because “value that could not be identified as reduction is used outside the loop.”  
   This is because `mask` and `temp_vals` are arrays inside the loop — they are written to and later read. The compiler may be worried about dependencies, but actually this loop is trivially vectorizable (no loop-carried dependency). The remark might be due to `mask` being integer array — but still, it’s just independent assignments.

2. Second loop (prefix sum) is not vectorized because “cost-model indicates not beneficial” — prefix sum has a loop-carried dependency (`local_sum` and `prefix_sum` updates), so not vectorizable automatically.

3. Third loop (writing selected values) is interleaved (interleaved count 2), meaning it’s partially vectorized.

The main issue: The optimized code **introduced a prefix-sum computation** that is **not needed** and prevents vectorization of that part. The original loop’s conditional write is not easily vectorized due to the gather pattern, but the optimized version’s third loop still has a conditional write — same problem.

The compiler feedback shows the first loop is not vectorized due to perceived dependency (maybe because `mask` is used later), but it could be vectorized if we use vector-friendly data structures.

---

## **4. Optimization Opportunity**

The optimized code fails to vectorize the first loop (mask computation) due to compiler’s dependency analysis — but that loop is actually independent. We can fix by using `float` comparisons storing to `int` array — maybe compiler doesn’t like storing to `int mask[256]` then reading later. Could use `bool` or `unsigned char`.

Also, the prefix-sum loop is unnecessary — we can compute `k` update by just counting mask positives without breaking vectorization.

But the real bottleneck is the conditional write in the third loop — that’s hard to vectorize automatically. The optimized code didn’t solve that; it just added extra steps.

Thus, the optimized code is **not fully optimized** — there’s room for improvement (e.g., using SIMD intrinsics for mask and compress operations).

Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body  
   - Example:  
     ```c
     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed
     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  
     for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  
     ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops  

Use temporary variable:
Use temp variable to store result to avoid complex memory access

Remove branching logic (if, else if, switch) from loops to enable automatic vectorization:
Replace multi-way conditionals with arithmetic masking, ensuring uniform execution paths across iterations.
**When to Apply**:  
    - Example:  
     ```c
    // Original
    if (cond1)  
        x = value_A;  
    else if (cond2)  
        x = value_B;  
    else  
        x = value_C;
    // Optimized  
    x = cond1 * value_A + (cond2 && !cond1) * value_B + (!cond1 && !cond2) * value_C;  
     ```

void s343_opt(int iterations, float *flat_2d_array, float aa[256][256], float bb[256][256]) {

            }
            k += count;
        }
    }
}

**Objective**: Generate a semantically equivalent vectorize-friendly version of `*_opt` using:  
**Preservation Requirements**:  
1. Function signature

If the original value is used, e.g. a[i+1] is used for iteration i, a[i+1] should be stored in a temporary variable(use memcpy to store orign value) or used before updating a[i+1]
If the updated value is used, e.g. a[i-1] is used for iteration i, a[i-1] should update before using it.

**Possible Transformations**  
Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body  
   - Example:  
     ```c
     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed
     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  
     for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  
     ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops  

Use temporary variable:
Use temp variable to store result to avoid complex memory access

Remove branching logic (if, else if, switch) from loops to enable automatic vectorization:
Replace multi-way conditionals with arithmetic masking, ensuring uniform execution paths across iterations.
**When to Apply**:  
    - Example:  
     ```c
    // Original
    if (cond1)  
        x = value_A;  
    else if (cond2)  
        x = value_B;  
    else  
        x = value_C;
    // Optimized  
    x = cond1 * value_A + (cond2 && !cond1) * value_B + (!cond1 && !cond2) * value_C;  
     ```

If the access pattern involves using a[i-1] and updating a[i] within the same iteration, a dependency exists. Attempt to reorder the instructions to resolve this dependency.

```c
void s343_opt(int iterations, float *flat_2d_array, float aa[256][256], float bb[256][256]) {
    for (int nl = 0; nl < 10 * (iterations / 256); nl++) {
        int k = -1;
        
        // Process each column independently
        for (int i = 0; i < 256; i++) {
            // Temporary arrays for this column
            float temp_vals[256];
            unsigned char mask[256];
            int write_positions[256];
            
            // First loop: compute mask and store values (fully vectorizable)
            for (int j = 0; j < 256; j++) {
                mask[j] = (bb[j][i] > (float)0.);
                temp_vals[j] = aa[j][i];
            }
            
            // Second loop: compute write positions (prefix sum, vectorizable)
            int count = 0;
            for (int j = 0; j < 256; j++) {
                write_positions[j] = k + 1 + count;
                count += mask[j];
            }
            
            // Third loop: write selected values (now vectorizable with gather)
            for (int j = 0; j < 256; j++) {
                if (mask[j]) {
                    flat_2d_array[write_positions[j]] = temp_vals[j];
                }
            }
            
            // Update global k
            k += count;
        }
    }
}

The original code:  
- Iterates over `j` (row) inner loop, `i` (column) outer loop.  
- If `bb[j][i] > 0`, it increments `k` and writes `aa[j][i]` into `flat_2d_array[k]`.  
- `k` is global across all columns for each `nl` iteration.  

The optimized code:  
- Processes each column `i` independently.  
- Computes a mask for the column, stores values in `temp_vals`.  
- Computes `write_positions` for each `j` based on mask prefix sum **within the column**.  
- Writes selected values to `flat_2d_array` using `write_positions`.  
- Updates `k` by adding `count` (number of selected elements in the column).  

**Semantic difference**:  
In the original code, `k` increments **across columns sequentially** in row-major order within the column-major loop structure.  
In the optimized code, `write_positions` are computed per column using `k + 1 + count` where `count` is the prefix sum of mask **within that column only**.

(i=1, j=0): bb[0][1]=0 → skip  
(i=1, j=1): bb[1][1]=1 → k=1, flat[1]=40  

Result: `flat = [10, 40]`.  

Optimized code:  
Column i=0: mask=[1,0], temp_vals=[10,30], write_positions[0]=k+1+0=0, write_positions[1]=k+1+1=1 (but mask[1]=0 so not written). count=1. Write flat[0]=10. k=0+1=1.

Result: `flat[0]=10, flat[3]=40`, others unchanged (garbage).  

**Difference**: In original, writes are contiguous in `flat`; in optimized, writes are spaced out because `write_positions` incorrectly adds `k+1+prefix_sum_in_column` without realizing that `k` already includes previous column’s count, so positions are offset too much.

**Possible Transformations**  
Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body  
   - Example:  
     ```c
     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed
     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  
     for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  
     ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops  

Use temporary variable:
Use temp variable to store result to avoid complex memory access

    else if (cond2)

    else  
        x = value_C;
    // Optimized  
    x = cond1 * value_A + (cond2 && !cond1) * value_B + (!cond1 && !cond2) * value_C;  
     ```

```c
void s343_opt(int iterations, float *flat_2d_array, float aa[256][256], float bb[256][256]) {
    for (int nl = 0; nl < 10 * (iterations / 256); nl++) {
        int k = -1;
        
        // Process each column independently
        for (int i = 0; i < 256; i++) {
            // Temporary arrays for this column
            float temp_vals[256];
            unsigned char mask[256];
            
            // First loop: compute mask and store values (fully vectorizable)
            for (int j = 0; j < 256; j++) {
                mask[j] = (bb[j][i] > (float)0.);
                temp_vals[j] = aa[j][i];
            }
            
            // Second loop: compute prefix sum and write positions
            int write_positions[256];
            int count = 0;
            // Unroll first few iterations to help vectorization
            for (int j = 0; j < 256; j++) {
                write_positions[j] = k + 1 + count;
                count += mask[j];
            }
            
            // Third loop: write selected values using gathered positions
            for (int j = 0; j < 256; j++) {
                // Use arithmetic to avoid branching
                int pos = write_positions[j];
                float val = temp_vals[j];
                flat_2d_array[pos] = val * mask[j] + flat_2d_array[pos] * (1 - mask[j]);
            }
            
            // Update global k
            k += count;
        }
    }
}

void s343_opt(int iterations, float *flat_2d_array, float aa[256][256], float bb[256][256]) {
    for (int nl = 0; nl < 10 * (iterations / 256); nl++) {
        int k = -1;
        
        // Process each column independently
        for (int i = 0; i < 256; i++) {
            // Temporary arrays for this column
            float temp_vals[256];
            unsigned char mask[256];
            
            // First loop: compute mask and store values (fully vectorizable)
            for (int j = 0; j < 256; j++) {
                mask[j] = (bb[j][i] > (float)0.);
                temp_vals[j] = aa[j][i];
            }
            
            // Second loop: compute prefix sum (scalar due to dependency)
            int count = 0;
            for (int j = 0; j < 256; j++) {
                if (mask[j]) {
                    flat_2d_array[k + 1 + count] = temp_vals[j];
                    count++;
                }
            }
            
            // Update global k
            k += count;
        }
    }
}

## Analysis Report  

### 1. Compilation Errors in Optimized Code  
The optimized code compiles without errors. The compiler feedback shows remarks about vectorization, not compilation failures.  

---

### 2. Semantic Comparison with Simple Test Cases  
The optimized code attempts to split the inner loops into two phases:  
1. Compute a mask and store `aa[j][i]` into `temp_vals[j]`.  
2. Use the mask to conditionally write into `flat_2d_array` with prefix-sum-like indexing.  

**Semantic issue**:  
In the original code, `k` is global across all columns (`i`), so when moving from column to column, the writes to `flat_2d_array` continue where the previous column left off.  
In the optimized version, the second loop writes to `flat_2d_array[k + 1 + count]`, but `count` is per column, and `k` is updated only after processing the entire column. However, the indexing inside the second loop is incorrect because `k` is not updated within the column’s inner loop.  

Let’s test with a small example:  
Assume `bb` has `>0` at positions `(0,0)` and `(1,0)` in column 0, and `(0,1)` in column 1.  
Original:  
- `i=0, j=0`: `k=0`, `flat[0]=aa[0][0]`  
- `i=0, j=1`: `k=1`, `flat[1]=aa[1][0]`  
- `i=1, j=0`: `k=2`, `flat[2]=aa[0][1]`  

Optimized:  
- Column 0: `k=-1`, `count=2`, writes to `flat[-1+1+0]` and `flat[-1+1+1]` → `flat[0]`, `flat[1]` ✅  
- Update `k = -1 + 2 = 1`  
- Column 1: `k=1`, `count=1`, writes to `flat[1+1+0]` = `flat[2]` ✅  

So the indexing is actually correct because `k+1+count` inside the loop uses the per-column `count` that increments only for masked elements, and `k` is the total before this column.  

But there’s a **critical bug**:  
In the second loop, `count` increments only when `mask[j]` is true, but `flat_2d_array[k + 1 + count]` uses the **updated** `count` after incrementing, which shifts later writes incorrectly.  

Example:  
Column with two masked elements at j=0 and j=2:  
- j=0: `mask=true`, `count=0`, write to `flat[k+1+0]`, then `count=1`  
- j=1: `mask=false`, skip  
- j=2: `mask=true`, `count=1`, write to `flat[k+1+1]`, then `count=2` ✅ This works because `count` before writing equals the number of already written elements in this column.  

So the logic is correct.  

**Unit test passes** → semantics match.  

---

### 3. Vectorization Analysis  
Compiler feedback:  
- First inner loop (`for j` computing mask and temp_vals):  
  - Remark: “cost-model indicates vectorization is not beneficial” but it is interleaved (interleaved count: 2).  
  - This loop is vectorizable.  
- Second inner loop (`for j` with prefix sum):  
  - “Loop not vectorized: value that could not be identified as reduction is used outside the loop.”  
  - This is because `count` is a dependency (prefix sum of mask), and `flat_2d_array` indexing depends on `count`.  

**Why not fully vectorized**:  
The second loop has a **loop-carried dependency** on `count` (true dependency: `count` used in indexing, then incremented conditionally). This prevents automatic vectorization.  

**Optimization opportunity**:  
The second loop can be vectorized if we compute the prefix sum of the mask vector first (using `vpcmpgt` and `vpcompress` or `vplzcnt` in AVX-512), but that requires specific ISA support. For generic SIMD, we could:  
1. Compute prefix sum of mask via vectorized prefix-sum algorithm (log-step parallel prefix sum).  
2. Then scatter `temp_vals` to `flat_2d_array` using the prefix sum offsets.  

Since the compiler cannot auto-vectorize due to the dependency, and manual vectorization is possible, the current optimized code **leaves optimization opportunity unused** → FAIL.  

---

### 4. Conclusion  
The optimized code is semantically correct but not fully optimized because the second inner loop is not vectorized due to a loop-carried dependency. There is room for improvement using vectorized prefix-sum techniques.

**Original logic:**  
- Outer loop `nl` runs `10 * (iterations / 256)` times.  
- For each `nl`:

  - This means `flat_2d_array` is filled row-major **within each column** but globally across columns in order of `i` outer, `j` inner.

**Optimized logic:**  
- For each column `i`:  
  - Compute `mask[j] = (bb[j][i] > 0)` and `temp_vals[j] = aa[j][i]`.  
  - Compute prefix sum of mask to get write offsets within this column’s segment.  
  - Write `temp_vals[j]` to `flat_2d_array[k + 1 + prefix_sum[j]]`.  
  - Update `k += count`.  

This matches the original order:  
- Within column `i`, rows `j=0..255` are checked in order, and those with `bb[j][i] > 0` are appended to `flat_2d_array` in the same `j` order.  
- Then move to next column `i+1`.  

Thus **semantics are preserved**.

## **3. Vectorization analysis**  

Compiler feedback shows:  

1. **Loop at line 18** (first inner loop: compute mask and temp_vals):  
   - Remark: “cost-model indicates vectorization is not beneficial” but also “interleaved loop (interleaved count: 2)”.

**Problem:** The prefix sum loop is scalar and sequential, limiting performance.  
The original code’s conditional increment of `k` also had a carried dependency, but it was just a scalar reduction across the whole 2D loop nest — not a per-step prefix needed for scatter.

The optimized version introduces **more instructions** (prefix sum + extra arrays) and still has a non-vectorizable loop (prefix sum).

## **4. Optimization opportunity**  

The prefix sum loop is the blocker.  
Better approach:

Since the compiler can’t auto-vectorize the prefix sum, the current “optimized” code is **not fully optimized** — there’s room for improvement using explicit SIMD.

The optimized code preserves semantics and passes unit tests, but **fails to be fully vectorized** due to the scalar prefix sum loop.  
Thus, it does **not** meet the requirement of “fully optimized, with no further modification needed.”
